公司簡介 
東陽成立於 1967 年，主營項目為汽、機車
零組件 ， 為台灣塑膠件供應商 ， 也是全球第

AM 營收佔比 75.68%，OEM 為 24.32%。

歐洲 11%、中東 3%。OEM 市場為中國
64%、台灣 36%。

汽車 10%、廣汽 4%

本次報告：電話訪談

東陽 (1319 TT) 
AM 產業長期正向

永豐觀點 
預估 2027 年 AM 營收增長 5~8%、OEM 年增 1~3%。 
 投資評價與建議 
維持買進建議 ：預估 2026、2027 年營收為 242.9、261 億元(-3.2%、+7.5%YoY)、
毛利率 32.7%、34%，營業利益為 40.5、49 億元(-12%、+21%YoY)，稅後淨利
為 36.1、40.2 億元(-4.9%、+11%YoY)，稅後 EPS 6.12、6.79 元。看好東陽最差
情況已過，即將迎來旺季，出貨將逐步重返正常增長軌道。目前評價交易在
13X2026 年 EPS(F)、11.9X2027 年 EPS(F)，為近五年本益比區間(10~18X)下緣，
本次以 2027 年 EPS 進行評價，給予 15X 本益比，目標價 102 元(15X2027 年(F) 
EPS 6.79 元)，維持買進建議。

ESG 評析 
東陽企業永續評鑑整體為 B+等，於 SASB 產業永續議題與跨產業五大永續面向平
均得分分別為 B+等與 B+等。

### Page 2

公司簡介：東陽成立於 1967 年，主營項目為汽、機車零組件，為台灣塑膠件供應
商，也是全球第一大汽車 AM 塑膠件製造商，塑膠件全球市佔率 70%、板金部份
市佔率 38%。2Q26 AM 營收佔比 75.68%，OEM 為 24.32%。AM 營收分布為北
美 71%、中南美 11%、歐洲 11%、中東 3%。OEM 市場為中國 64%、台灣 36%。
1995 年起，公司陸續在中國重慶、南京、福州、長春、哈爾濱、敦陽、長春、天
津、武漢、長沙、廣州等地設廠，中國 OEM(非合併)主要客戶包含一汽 64%、特
斯拉 11%、長安汽車 10%、廣汽 4%。 
關稅底定，北美 AM 客戶回補庫存，3Q26 AM 營收年增 15.7%，重回成長：美
國 5 月下旬將台灣輸美汽車零件的 232 條款關稅調降至 15% 後，客戶結束觀
望，回補先前壓低的庫存，拉貨逐月增加，目前東陽工廠稼動率逾 90%，採兩班
制 24 小時生產。3Q26 AM 營收 45 億元(+5% QoQ、+15.7% YoY)，9 月 AM 營
收 14.96 億元(+0.85% MoM、+7.6% YoY)。 
AM 產業長期正面：北美維修量降幅收斂、副廠件占比創高；北美旺季即將到
來，客戶補庫存可期：北美碰撞維修量已從 2025 年的大幅衰退回到接近持平，
副廠件占比創新高，產業面已落底，結構變化對東陽長期有利： 
1. 汽車維修產業面：維修量衰退已收斂，且AM 件占比創新高：依美國理賠估價
平台 CCC Intelligent Solutions 統計(下簡稱 CCC)，2026 年 1~9 月全美理
賠件數降幅由去年同期的 8.3%縮小至 3.5%，可修復估價件數由 10.4%縮小
至 5.4%。北美大型汽車維修連鎖企業 Boyd 估計 2Q26 可修復理賠量持平至
年減 2%，與 CCC 方向一致。另，依 CCC 統計，2025 年副廠件占全美碰撞
換件金額 22.7%，為歷史新高；在維修量下滑的環境下，副廠件金額仍成長  
1.7%，原廠件則減少 1.8%。綜合上述，產業面已落底，有望回溫，而結構方
面，副廠件使用率持續提升，東陽為受惠者。 
2. 東陽客戶面：主要客戶 LKQ 於北美 AM 碰撞件優於市場：LKQ 2Q26 北美
零件及服務有機營收成長 0.5%，為九季來首度轉正，其中 AM 碰撞件營收
成長約 2%；同時，LKQ 公布其替代零件使用率(APU，即維修時選用副廠件、
而非原廠件的比例)連續兩季創下歷史新高，已突破 40%，背後原因是美國保
險公司近期理賠成本上升壓力較大，加上二手車價格走高，促使保險公司更傾

營運現況與分析 
圖一：東陽主要生產汽車塑膠及鈑金件

資料來源：東陽，Oct. 2026

### Page 3

3

向要求維修廠使用價格較低的副廠零件以控制成本 ， 顯示副廠零件滲透率提升
是長期趨勢，而非短期景氣波動。 
3. 研究部研判：AM 最差情況已過。維修量降幅收斂、副廠件占比提升，加上關
稅底定後的補庫存，再加上東陽每年投入平均約 18 億元開發新模具(今年為
25 億元)、持續擴大北美與歐洲保險公司認證，支撐 AM 營收回到成長軌道、
長期向好。惟 4Q25 受暴風雪帶動、基期較高，若今年冬天偏暖，則 4Q26
年增幅度可能有限；1Q27 則因 1Q26 低基期，年增表現較有利。 
短期負面因子：貨櫃仍偏緊，緩解速度取決於旺季強度：目前待出貨訂單累積 200
多櫃訂單，等待客戶安排貨櫃艙位，緩解速度需視旺季強度(採 FOB 報價，過往客
戶在需求好的時候願意加價取得貨櫃)。研究處認為目前客戶庫存約 1 個月，且旺
季(11 月~隔年 3 月)到來，預期有望逐步緩解。 
4Q26 毛利率有機會優於 3Q26：旺季稼動率有機會達 90%~95%(目前東陽工廠
稼動率逾 90%)，可攤提模具折舊等固定成本(AM 模具折舊約占 AM 成本 17%)。
旺季時北美銷售比重由淡季約 65%~66%回升至約 70%，有助產品組合。而市場
擔憂原物料上升的部分，截至九月來看，PP 較戰前上漲 5%，ABS 為 24%，但因
塑膠原料約 90%使用 PP、ABS 僅約 10%， 且東陽議價能力強 ， 成本上漲可轉嫁，
今年以來東陽產品售價已調整約 1.1%~1.5%，占比近 30%的自有產品轉嫁較快，
其餘產品約需一季~半年轉嫁，綜合以上，原物料上漲的部分對整體毛利率影響不
大。主要影響毛利率的因素在於產品組合(北美營收佔比、塑膠件比重)，以及稼動
率，故預期 4Q26、1Q27AM 毛利率將有望回升至 40%以上。 
預估 2027 年東陽 AM 營收重回正常成長軌道，年增 5~8%；OEM 年增 1~3%：
東陽是全球第一大汽車 AM 塑膠件製造商，塑膠件全球市佔率 70%、板金部份市
佔率 38%，其品項覆蓋範圍最廣，且模具持續增加，故在市況不明朗時，東陽仍
佔據產業領先地位及強議價能力，東陽2026 年預估將有 500~600 項 AM 新品通
過認證，新產品毛利率較高，搭配 Q4~Q1 迎來傳統旺季，預估 AM 營收有望持
續回溫。AM 市場的競爭重點便是模具數及規模經濟，參考東陽於 2024-2025 年
擴大模具開發套數及擴充產能，將有助於未來幾年 AM 營收的增長。而 OEM 方
面，台灣車市有望逐步復甦，且東陽客戶新案陸續放量，有望支撐2027 年營運表
現。中國 OEM 方面，今明年重點在於電動車轉型持續發酵，客戶展望正面，雖然
前幾年陸續關廠導致營收規模持續縮減， 但近期已調整告一段落， 預期整體產能利
用率能夠提升，推升毛利率表現。綜合以上，研究部預估2027 年東陽 AM 營收重
回正常成長軌道，年增 5~8%；OEM 年增 1~3%。 
維持買進建議，長線投資價值顯現：預估 2026、2027 年營收為 242.9、261 億元
(-3.2%、+7.5%YoY)、毛利率 32.7%、34%，營業利益為 40.5、49 億元(-12%、
+21%YoY)，稅後淨利為 36.1、40.2 億元(-4.9%、+11%YoY)，稅後 EPS 6.12、
表一：北美維修量止跌、副廠件占比創高

## Document 049: 2026年10月5日國際經濟金融週報

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年10月5日國際經濟金融週報.pdf
Report source: Mega Bank (國際經濟金融週報)
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

本報告係根據研究資料及各項可靠數據作成，兆豐銀行保留報告內容所有權利，未經本行許可，請勿引
用相關內容。

兆豐國際商業銀行 徵信處 
中華民國一一五年十月五日

國際經濟金融週報

### Page 2

本報告係根據研究資料及各項可靠數據作成，兆豐銀行保留報告內容所有權利，未經本行許可，請勿引
用相關內容。

1

國際經濟金融週報內容摘要 
美 
國 
美國經濟維持溫和擴張，勞動市場降溫與通膨黏著牽動聯準會政策 
經濟 
⚫ 2026 年第 2 季美國實質 GDP 季增年率終值上修至 2.2%， 惟低於第1 季
之 2.5%。成長主要由個人消費及固定投資帶動，其中消費貢獻 2.51 個
百分點；惟進口大增 12.6%，致淨出口拖累 1.10 個百分點。另實質私人
國內最終銷售成長 4.6%，顯示內需仍具韌性。 
⚫ 9 月美國非農就業僅增 2.9 萬人，遠低於前月及近一年平均，失業率升
至 4.2%， 薪資增幅續降 ， 顯示企業聘僱動能轉弱 。 惟勞動市場尚未明顯
惡化，後續仍須關注就業降溫對聯準會利率決策之影響。 
⚫ 8 月美國職位空缺數由 733.5 萬個降至 707.9 萬個，職缺率降至 4.3%，
顯示企業徵才需求略為降溫；惟聘僱人數增至 519.2 萬人。主動離職及
裁員規模同步下降，反映勞工轉職意願與企業人力調整趨於審慎，勞動
市場供需大致維持平衡。 
⚫ 9 月美國 ISM 製造業採購經理人指數為 54.5，連續第九個月維持擴張。
新訂單、就業及未完成訂單同步改善，顯示需求仍具韌性；惟生產、出
口及進口動能略為放緩，且原物料成本持續攀升，加上關稅政策、地緣
政治及供應鏈不確定性仍存，製造業仍面臨部分壓力。 
⚫ 8 月美國 PCE 物價指數月增 0.3%、年增 3.4%，核心 PCE 月增 0.2%、
年增 3.0%， 顯示通膨大致持穩但短期物價壓力略升 。 另一方面 ， 個人消
費支出月增 0.9%、實質消費成長 0.6%，帶動儲蓄率降至 4.1%，顯示家
庭需求仍具韌性；後續須關注消費熱度及通膨對聯準會政策之影響。 
重要 
事件 
⚫ 近期聯準會官員立場趨於分歧，理事 Michael Barr 認為能源價格及 AI
投資熱潮可能延緩通膨降溫 ， 未來仍可能進一步升息 ； 惟John Williams、
Philip Jefferson 及 Michelle Bowman 等官員則主張暫無急於升息的必要 ，
傾向觀察後續經濟數據。加以 9 月非農就業明顯放緩，市場對 10 月升
息預期大幅降溫，焦點轉向 12 月會議。 
⚫ 台積電傳評估與馬斯克旗下 Terafab 合作赴德州設廠，雙方仍處討論階
段。合作可能由台積電主導營運，或由 Terafab 持多數股權、台積電提
供技術 。 若成真 ， 將擴大台積電美國布局 ， 並支援Tesla、SpaceX 及 xAI
晶片需求。

一、 美國—美國經濟維持溫和擴張，勞動市場降溫與通膨黏著牽動聯準會政策 
2026 年第二季美國實質 GDP 季增年率終值由前次估計之 1.5%上修至 2.2%，
惟仍低於第一季之 2.5%， 顯示經濟持續擴張但成長動能略有放緩 。 此次上修主要
反映投資、消費及政府支出優於原先估計。就成長結構觀察，個人消費支出仍為
主要動能，貢獻 2.51 個百分點，其中服務及商品消費分別貢獻 1.57 及 0.94 個百
分點；私人投資貢獻 0.82 個百分點，以設備投資及智慧財產權投資表現強勁，惟
庫存調整拖累 0.53 個百分點。對外貿易方面，出口成長 5.0%，但進口大幅增加
12.6%， 致淨出口拖累GDP 成長 1.10 個百分點 。 另反映內需基本面的實質私人國
內最終銷售成長 4.6%， 高於第一季之1.8%， 顯示民間需求明顯增強 。 物價方面 ，
第 2 季 PCE 及核心 PCE 物價指數分別上升 5.0%及 3.3%， 顯示通膨壓力仍偏高 。
整體而言，美國經濟仍由消費與固定投資支撐，惟進口快速增加及通膨黏著性，
仍為後續經濟前景的重要觀察重點。 
2026 年 9 月美國非農就業人數增加 2.9 萬人 ， 明顯低於上月修正後的13.3 萬
人，也低於過去 12 個月平均每月增加 4.5 萬人的水準；失業率小幅升至 4.2%，
整體仍維持在 3 月以來 4.1%至 4.3%的區間，反映勞動市場整體變化有限，惟新
增就業力道已轉趨疲弱。家庭調查方面，勞動參與率升至 61.8%，就業人口比為
59.2%，均較上月小幅上升；長期失業人數約 194.4 萬人，占總失業人口 27.1%，
大致維持穩定。產業別方面，主要產業就業變化有限，其中醫療保健增加 1.7 萬
人、營建業增加 1.1 萬人、製造業增加 0.9 萬人，金融活動則減少 0.7 萬人，政府
部門減少 1.7 萬人。薪資方面，私人部門平均時薪升至 37.81 美元，月增 0.1%、
年增 3.0%，平均每週工時維持 34.4 小時。另 7 月與 8 月新增就業合計下修 6 萬
人，反映前二月就業動能較初估疲弱。整體而言，9 月美國新增就業明顯放緩、
失業率小幅上升且薪資增速降溫，企業聘僱動能較先前轉弱；惟失業率仍維持於
近期區間，勞動市場尚未出現明顯惡化跡象，後續仍須關注企業聘僱需求是否持
續降溫，以及對聯準會利率決策的影響。 
2026 年 8 月美國職位空缺數(Job Openings) 由上月修正後之 733.5 萬個減少
25.6 萬個至 707.9 萬個 ， 職缺率由4.4%降至 4.3%， 顯示企業用人需求略有降溫 ；

惟聘僱人數由 514.6 萬人小幅增加至 519.2 萬人，聘僱率由 3.2%升至 3.3%。總離
職人數由 512.8 萬人減少至 507.0 萬人，離職率維持於 3.2%。就離職結構觀察，
主動離職(Quits)人數由 308.9 萬人降至 306.6 萬人，主動離職率維持 1.9%；裁員
與解僱(Layoffs and Discharges) 人數則由 170.2 萬人降至 164.1 萬人，裁員率由
1.1%降至 1.0%。產業別方面，職位空缺增加主要來自零售業、金融保險業、資訊
業及休閒餐飲業 ； 聘僱人數則以政府部門及製造業增幅較為明顯 。 整體而言 ，8 月
美國職缺數較前月下滑，反映企業徵才需求略有降溫，但聘僱活動仍維持穩定，
且主動離職與裁員規模同步下降，顯示勞工轉職意願及企業人力調整趨於審慎，
勞動市場供需仍大致維持平衡。 
2026 年 9 月美國 ISM 製造業採購經理人指數(Manufacturing PMI) 為 54.5，
較上月之 54.6 微降 0.1 點，惟已連續第九個月維持於 50 以上擴張區間，顯示製
造業景氣持續擴張。細項觀察，新訂單指數由53.7 升至 55.3，需求動能續增；生
產指數由 58.3 降至 56.7，雖仍維持擴張，但成長速度略有放緩；就業指數由51.2
升至 52.7，反映企業聘僱需求持續增加。供應商交貨指數由 59.3 微降至 59.0，交
貨延遲情況仍存；庫存指數則由 50.6 降至 48.6，企業備貨態度轉趨保守。此外，
價格指數由 71.1 升至 77.9，原物料及投入成本壓力升高；未完成訂單指數由51.8
升至 56.4，訂單去化變慢。另一方面，新出口訂單指數由53.2 降至 50.9，進口指
數亦由 52.5 降至 51.0，外需成長動能及企業備料需求略為放緩。整體而言，9 月
美國製造業活動持續穩健擴張，新訂單、就業及未完成訂單同步改善，顯示製造
業景氣仍具韌性；惟出口及進口增速放緩、投入成本壓力升高，加上關稅政策、
地緣政治風險及供應鏈不確定性仍存，後續發展仍值得持續關注。 
2026 年 8 月美國個人消費支出(PCE)物價指數月增率由上月之 0.1%升至 0.3%，
年增率維持於 3.4%； 扣除食品及能源後之核心PCE 月增率由 0.1%升至 0.2%，年
增率亦持平於 3.0%，顯示整體通膨大致持穩，惟短期物價漲勢較前月略有回升。
另一方面，個人所得月增 0.2%，可支配所得(DPI)月增 0.3%，實質可支配所得持
平；個人消費支出月增 0.9%，實質個人消費支出成長 0.6%，顯示民間消費表現
明顯回溫。由於消費增幅高於所得增幅，個人儲蓄率由前月之 4.6%降至 4.1%。
整體而言，8 月美國通膨表現大致持穩，但消費支出顯著回升，反映家庭需求仍
具韌性；後續仍須持續關注消費熱度、通膨走勢及其對聯準會貨幣政策路徑之影
響。

近期聯準會官員談話呈現鷹鴿分歧。聯準會理事 Michael Barr 認為，中東局
勢推升能源價格，以及 AI 相關投資熱潮帶動需求增長，均可能阻礙通膨回落至
2%目標 ， 未來仍不排除進一步升息的必要性 ； 惟 紐約聯準銀行總裁 John Williams、
聯準會副主席 Philip Jefferson 及金融監理副主席 Michelle Bowman 則強調，在 9
月升息後，當前並無急迫性立即進一步收緊政策，應先觀察後續通膨、就業及金
融市場數據變化，再評估是否有進一步調整利率的必要。受官員談話轉趨審慎，
以及 9 月非農就業表現疲弱影響，市場對 10 月 FOMC 會議再度升息的預期明顯
降溫，政策焦點逐漸轉向 12 月會議及年底前是否仍有一次升息空間。 
晶圓代工龍頭台積電傳出正評估與馬斯克旗下 Terafab 合作，在美國德州布
局晶圓廠，馬斯克也於社群平台 X 證實雙方正在討論，並表示 「目前僅在討論階
段 ， 但可能會有進展」 。 目前浮上檯面的合作模式包括由台積電持有並營運新廠 ，
SpaceX 或 Terafab 透過入股及承諾採購晶圓參與；另一方案則由 Terafab 掌握多
數股權 ， 台積電提供製程技術與晶圓廠營運經驗 。Terafab 旨在因應 Tesla、SpaceX
及 xAI 快速成長的 AI 晶片需求，首期投資規模達 168 億美元。若合作成真，不
僅可望成為台積電繼亞利桑那州後在美國的新布局，也凸顯先進製程與穩定晶片
供應的重要性，但目前具體合作方式、時程與投資內容仍未正式定案。 
二、歐元區—法國殖利率大幅上升，不利於投資及消費表現 
 歐元區 9 月 HICP 通膨 、 核心通膨(不含食品及能源)分別上升 0.6、0.1 個百分
點至 3.8%、2.5%， 主因國際原油價格上漲 ， 使能源通膨上升4.5 個百分點至 18.8%，
並透過肥料、物流成本上漲，間接推升食品通膨 0.3 個百分點至 1.4%。另外，服
務通膨上升 0.2 個百分點至 3.2%，近三個月服務通膨大致呈現橫向波動，部分反
映旅遊旺季因素影響。基於布蘭特原油持續位於每桶 90 美元以上，預期年底前
仍將對通膨帶來額外壓力，後續須關注能源漲價可能會透過運輸、物流等成本管
道傳導至核心商品及服務價格，進而提高核心通膨黏性。 
 其他經濟數據包含 ：(1)歐元區 8 月失業率持平 6.4%， 失業人數增加2.6 萬人 ，
主要是義大利勞動參與人數增加所致，不過 ，基於PMI 顯示近期德國勞動雇用需
求持續增溫，將有助於穩定勞動市場表現；(2)歐元區第二季家庭儲蓄率下降 0.2
個百分點至 14.2%，顯示通膨壓力削弱民眾購買力，致儲蓄暫時性減少；(3)歐元
區第二季房價年增率為 4%， 較第一季降0.6 個百分點 ， 主因法國中古屋面臨嚴格

環保法規，須負擔高昂翻修成本，使法國房價呈現連三季下滑所拖累。 
 近一週法國 10 年期公債殖利率升至 4.9%以上，與德國公債殖利率差距擴大
至 125 個基點以上，主要反映市場對法國主權公債可持續性疑慮升溫。儘管目前
政府計畫將 2027 年財政赤字縮限至 5%，惟政府在國會並無穩定多數席次，即使
能以行政命令強行通過預算 ， 也可能面臨倒閣風險 ；此外 ，市場對於2027 年 4 月
總統大選後，新總統能否取得國會足夠支持，推動削減赤字與控制債務的改革亦
缺乏信心。因此未來法國仍可能面臨殖利率進一步上升與財政壓力加劇的風險，
暫時不利於投資及消費信心。 
三、日本—景氣溫和復甦，但消費放緩與通膨升溫風險升高 
 8 月工業生產月減 1.7%，主要係受到該月地震及豪雨等天然災害影響，導致
部分工廠暫時停產。從細項來看，汽車業受停工影響最為明顯，產量下滑6.8%，
電子產品產量亦減少 1.9%。 惟生產機械產量連續三個月成長 ， 加上核心資本財出
貨持續增加，顯示企業投資需求仍相當強勁，有助於支撐後續工業生產回升。從
企業預期來看，製造商預期9 月產量將成長 3.2%，顯示生產活動可望迅速回溫，
在供應鏈逐步恢復、庫存回補需求持續，以及穩健的企業資本支出支撐下，預計
第三季工業生產仍可維持正成長，且成長動能可望延續至年底。 
 8 月零售月減 1.2%，雖然表現略優於市場預期，但仍顯示民間消費動能正在
放緩。此次疲弱主要原因來自耐久財銷售下滑，其中家電銷售因空調等產品在換
機需求告一段落後明顯回落，汽車銷售也隨政府刺激措施效應逐漸消退而連續第
四個月下降。相較之下，食品、服裝等非耐久財銷售雖同步走弱，但降幅相對有
限 ， 反映薪資成長仍對消費提供部分支撐 。惟不利天候與通膨壓力仍是主要風險 ，
8 月至 9 月持續豪雨已影響服裝等非必需消費，而除米價以外的食品及商品價格
持續上漲，也正侵蝕家庭實質購買力，恐限制消費者信心與消費復甦力道。 
 9 月東京核心通膨年增率升至 2.7%，雖然東京都免費托兒及免費水費政策造
成的基期效應貢獻約 0.5 個百分點，但通膨升溫主因仍是價格上漲已由能源與食
品擴散至整體經濟。受石油價格上揚帶動，企業開始加速將原物料及薪資成本轉
嫁給消費者，帶動商品與服務價格普遍走高；租金通膨更升至 2%，創 1994 年以
來新高，公共交通及醫療等項目也開始調漲。隨著東京的物價壓力逐步反映至全
國數據，日本央行進一步升息的可能性升高。

四、中國—為刺激內需、引導產業走向，人行漸近式放寬政策工具 
9 月官方製造業 PMI 上升 0.3 點至 50.1，為近三個月高點，新訂單、出口訂
單指數分別為 50.5、50，並帶動生產指數上升至 51.7，不過，庫存指數下降、就
業指數加速衰退、企業展望減弱，皆拖累 PMI 指數水準。此外，投入、產出物價
指數分別上升至 60.8、54，顯示成本壓力加劇，但售價調整速度依然較為遲緩，
不利於獲利表現。官方非製造業 PMI 上升 1.2 點至 50.2，其中建築業活動指數大
增 3.4 點至 50.3，脫離連續八個月的衰退區間，主要是政府加大財政刺激力度，
推動重大建設開工；非製造業的從業人員指數為 45.9，顯示就業情況仍顯嚴峻，
亦較不利於內需表現。 
鑒於內需動能疲弱，人行於 9 月 29 日宣布下調抵押補充貸款(該貸款本質是
將資金引導至政府希望發展的領域)利率自 1.75%降至 1.50%，並擴大適用範圍，
將水網、新型電網、算力網、新一代通信網、城市地下管網及物流網等建設納入
支持，有助於提振內需動能。未來若內需復甦力道不如預期，不排除人行將進一
步降準或擴大結構性貨幣工具支持經濟；至於全面降息則仍須兼顧人民幣匯率及
中美利差等因素，可能性相對較低。 
五、印度及東南亞—泰國、菲律賓出口均達 2 成以上的高速成長 
(1)新加坡：受服務業、零售暨其他商品業，以及食品價格漲幅擴大影響，8 月消

條件不佳影響，預期新加坡進口食品、商品及服務成本將持續上升，惟國內生產
率成長及政府補貼政策將抑制部分服務業通膨，新加坡金管局(MAS)或在 10 月

創兩年新高，主要得益於電子產品（年增 59.9%） ，尤其是半導體（73.5%）及黃

## Document 259: AI boom or doom_ what will superintelligence feel like and when will we get there_ (2026-10-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/AI boom or doom_ what will superintelligence feel like and when will we get there_ (2026-10-12).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-12
Pages: 20
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

AI boom or doom: what will 
superintelligence feel like and  
when will we get there?

1 
Authors 
Adrian Cox 
Research Analyst

Azeem Azhar, Exponential View 
Expert Collaborator

October 12, 2026 
The recent panic about rogue AI agents collaborating in swarms to take over the 
internet and threaten human existence has, more than occasionally, lacked 
nuance. But it does reflect how artificial intelligence is progressing in creative 
and surprising ways, and at a pace that seems uncontrollable. 
For some in the industry, it is evidence that the technology is following a path of 
accelerating self-improvement that will lead to artificial general intelligence (AGI) 
and, perhaps, to utopia or catastrophe. 
Former Google CEO Eric Schmidt has sketched out a “San Francisco 
Consensus” based on a belief in scaling laws, with ever larger AI models 
becoming an input into their own improvement; superintelligence by the end of 
the decade; and transformative AI systems outperforming humans on many 
tasks. This loop would lead to exponential growth in scientific advancement, 
financial returns and human progress. And crucially, AGI, and then 
superintelligence, would merely be milestones in a process, far from its climax. 
If that were the case, superintelligence could make much of the cognitive work 
that dominates the world economy much cheaper and change the ways in which 
firms compete. But its economic effects will depend on how businesses and 
customers respond. 
In this paper, we analyse what the San Francisco Consensus means for investors 
and enterprises in the years to come and what could accelerate – or slow down – 
its timeline of AGI by 2030. 
1. What would superintelligence look and feel like? 
2. Can we get there theoretically? 
3. Can we get there practically? 
4. What happens if we don’t get there? The base case 
The question is urgent. 
And it is complicated by reports of AI models autonomously accessing the 
internet and hacking other companies; a public backlash over the energy-
intensive data centres springing up as part of a historic $1 trillion a year 
investment boom; and dire warnings of threats to life if superintelligent AI runs 
roughshod over its human creators to achieve its own goals.

### Page 2

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 2

1. What would superintelligence look and feel like? 
1.1 What are AGI and superintelligence? 
Artificial general intelligence (AGI) is a simple milestone scribbled in pencil: an AI 
system that has surpassed human capabilities. Superintelligence is the 
continuum beyond that point. It is not merely systems that exceed the best 
human performance across one cognitive task or another, but system s that 
exceed human potential across every cognitive task. 
The San Francisco Consensus takes this further: the intelligence must be able to 
produce more intelligence. The key to getting there is so -called recursive self -
improvement, where the AI is able to autonomously design and improve itself in 
a way that compounds exponentially. A first step has maybe already been taken, 
with OpenAI saying recently that AI is now writing approximately 80 percent of 
the company’s code. 
There is significant disagreement on how we will be able to identify AGI or 
superintelligence when we see it, let alone what comes afterwards.  
OpenAI’s Charter defines AGI in economic terms, as “highly autonomous systems 
that outperform humans at the most economically valuable work”. Google 
researchers proposed a five -step ladder based on performance and generality, 
with level 5 “superhuman” for a system that outperforms 100% of humans. And 
philosopher Nick Bostrom, who got the ball rolling in 2014 with his influential 
book “Superintelligenc e”, defined it as “any intellect that greatly exceeds the 
cognitive performance of humans in virtually all domains of interest”. 
It’s also a moving target.  
In August, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said the company would have an internal 
system that he would call AGI by later this year but subsequently told a podcast 
that “at best it’s a very poorly defined term. I was going to say it’s like an irrelevant 
marketing term”. Yet his No. 2 Greg Brockman described the new Astra model at 
the start of September as being potentially the moment that “AGI was created”. 
For this report, we have chosen to focus on a system that outperforms essentially 
all humans across essentially all cognitive tasks, including the development of AI 
itself.  
Under our scenario, by 2030 the best performing AI models would have the 
qualities of “superintelligence”, that is better than human experts in all domains. 
These would be frontier models: and using them would feel exactly like bringing 
the best human experts in a room to solve whatever business problem you faced.

And the equivalents of today’s frontier models would be available, quite literally, 
everywhere, compressed enough to run on every phone, tablet or laptop 
computer.  
In early 2026, we already see early examples of what highly capable AI can do. 
They give a flavour for a world with superintelligence even if the reality would be 
a leap beyond, both quantitatively and qualitatively. At that point, 
superintelligence would mark a regime break, not a faster version of what we 
already have. Rather than blindly doing what humans specify, but just a bit better, 
it would specify the task itself.

### Page 3

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 3

Figure 1: What changes when cognitive work gets abundantly cheaper?

Source: Deutsche Bank Research

Meanwhile cost may be less and less of a constraint as ever greater machine 
intelligence becomes cheaply available. Of measured technologies, no 
technology has got cheaper faster than AI has, almost halving in price every 
quarter for the past three years on a like-for-like basis, or 13 times per year, Epoch 
data show. 
If these systems can be supplied widely, firms will be able to buy more of the 
complex, expensive and rare analysis, design and engineering kinds of services 
than they do today. Activities that were previously uneconomic could become 
viable, and in a competitive market, savings could pass to customers. The main 
driver will then be whether demand grows fast enough to preserve margins. 
However, superior capability, doesn’t, on its own, automatically lead to the 
adoption of cheap, abundant intelligence. Tasks that do not yet yield to super-

### Page 4

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 4

intelligence, perhaps because they require real world-coordination, will therefore 
end up commanding significant premia. 
At that point, cognitive labour stops being scarce and the bottleneck becomes 
the physical and practical assets that cannot be conjured by up more compute, 
such as electricity, land, steel and permits. 
1.2 Capability and diffusion 
If AI becomes capable of improving itself, the technology may compound faster 
and faster. The supply of machine intelligence could grow faster than business 
and physical infrastructure can accommodate.  
This balance between capability and diffusion creates different scenarios.

Figure 2: The balance between capabilities and diffusion

Source: Deutsche Bank Research

Lower capability, constrained diffusion: where we are now 
Progress is steady and sector-by-sector. This is how technology usually diffuses, 
according to researchers for the New York Fed back in 2003, who tracked 20 
major technologies across leading industrial economies from 1788 and found “a 
pattern of trickle-down diffusion that is remarkably robust across technologies”. 
Lower capability, high diffusion: the cloud transition 
This is faster because the complementary investment is small, with no retooling 
and no skills at scale required. Amazon Web Services launched the modern cloud 
in 2006, offering scalable computing and storage infrastructure. Adoption grew 
and then accelerated over the pandemic. Almost all enterprises now use it  in 
some form.

### Page 5

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 5

Higher capability, lower diffusion: the overhang 
This is theoretically possible and arguably where we are today in narrow domains. 
The technology would be applied in some sectors of the economy, while other 
sectors would move at a slower speed.  Superintelligent or not, it risks being 
bogged down by physical constraints, bureaucracy and human inertia. 
Higher capability, higher diffusion: superintelligence 
In this scenario, capability becomes so high that it changes the dynamics of 
diffusion itself. AI generates not just better AI (so -called recursive self -
improvement, or RSI) but also speeds up the integration process. 
Rapid AI diffusion could work two ways. 
In the first, AI could be enlisted to do integration work itself: mapping processes, 
rebuilding data work, retraining staff, writing the control and audit layer, and so 
on. The problem with this is that absorbing and recreating undocumented 
knowledge is impossible by definition, at least for a task-based superintelligence.  
The second way that higher capability AI could be rapidly diffused is much more 
competitive – and disruptive. Rather than incumbents slowly converting AI, 
perhaps using it for some of the hard work of integration, they could be instead 
displaced by new firms that have been built AI-native from day one. New entrants 
will never have to incur integration costs because there is nothing to integrate. 
Incumbents will need to change – fast – or die.  
Change could also be forced by earlier adopters in the supply chain, as happened 
with one company after another adopting electronic data interchange (EDI) to 
link with its suppliers or customers, or by a migration of capital towards early 
adopters, reprici ng the cost of capital for slow -coaches and prodding them to 
move faster. 
1.3 The forcing mechanism: change or die 
A company that uses superintelligence may outperform one that doesn’t. 
Firms using superintelligence will likely have substantial cost and revenue -
harvesting advantages. Every firm will therefore have to adopt superintelligence 
or retrench to a smaller set of offerings. And any business line of lasting value 
abandoned by a no n-adopter will be taken over by an adopter. Customer inertia 
will provide a safe harbour for a limited time only.  
The analogy here is electricity. There came a point in the deployment of 
electricity where firms in every sector, from entertainment to finance, from 
healthcare to transport, had to adopt the technology to remain relevant.  
Under this scenario, wait -and-see will not be an option. There will be no stable 
“non-adoption” alternative. Sufficiently high capabilities will force a tipping point 
of rapid diffusion and competitive repricing. Firms using yesterday’s production 
model will either have to change, choose a different market or die.  
Of course, caveats apply.  
Customers may not change behaviour immediately. They may even prefer 
companies which limit their use of AI, in the same way that some customers 
choose to avoid factory-produced food.

### Page 6

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 6

The paradox that Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Solow observed in 1987, 
that “you can see the computer age everywhere but in the productivity statistics”, 
is likely to still apply for a while – even with superintelligence. 
The reason is that the productivity J -curve identified by Stanford University AI 
economy guru Erik Brynjolfsson et al is unavoidable. In that scenario, the drag of 
early intangible investments to integrate general purpose technologies means 
that productivity initially appears to slow before rapidly accelerating.

Figure 3: Productivity J-curve: slow then fast

Source: Brynjolfsson E, Rock D, Syverson C, “The Productivity J-Curve” (2018, revised 2020), Deutsche Bank Research

Furthermore, “change or die” would require abundant capital and low regulatory 
entry points for nimble new entrants. But in practice, highly regulated areas such 
as banking, utilities, pharmaceuticals and defence are tough nuts to crack. 
Existing contracts and licences, high switching costs and decades of depreciated 
capital investment and infrastructure would shield incumbents at least for a 
healthy transition period. 
For an extreme example from the real world of better, cheaper technology failing 
to force change, take Pakistan’s 3.6 -gigawatt imported coal power plant fleet 
installed in the mid -2010s. In that case, it came with expensive take -or-pay 
liabilities which wi ll continue to run for decades, even though changes like 
cheaper rooftop solar have cut utilisation to below 20 percent.  
So: superintelligence will not force adoption everywhere. It will hit hardest in 
contestable sectors, say, high -volume customer services, routine software 
engineering and freelance creative work, and widen the gap between them and 
protected ones. Forced adoption will be about relative value, not an apocalypse.

### Page 7

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 7

1.4 What survives and what it means for portfolios 
Because AI sits on an aggressive learning curve, its price on an equivalent quality 
basis is plummeting. In a world of recursive super intelligence this is likely to 
continue. Its price will fall to the cost of compute.  
What will retain its value will be whatever the model cannot replicate: 
1. Proprietary data 
2. The right to operate , particularly in heavily regulated sectors such as 
healthcare and finance. 
3. Sticky customer relationships,  such as those where mission -critical 
workflows and databases are deeply entwined. 
4. Excludable physical assets, such as machinery and land. 
5. In addition, humans have proven to have unlimited wants.  Each shift in 
technological capabilities or cultural norms has delivered new needs to 
be met. Some will be needs we can articulate today, such as originality or 
empathy, but new ones are likely to arise.  
If this forcing mechanism holds, firms will separate into two “bimodal” groups: 
adopters and casualties, with very little in between. 
This dispersion of performance may create challenges for investors. The four 
years since the launch of ChatGPT in November 2022 have seen the stocks of 
semiconductor and semiconductor equipment makers surge almost seven times 
while those of software makers  have only risen by 80 percent. Conventional  
diversification and volatility-based allocation may become ineffective strategies.

Figure 4: Total return for S&P 500 semiconductor vs software stocks since 
launch of ChatGPT on Nov 30, 2022

Source: Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research

### Page 8

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 8

Diversification normally works because idiosyncratic risks are unrelated and 
average each other out. Here they would not: with one variable that overrides 
everything else, spreading capital across more names would just spread exposure 
rather than diluting it. 
Investors will need to consider adapting their approach in three ways: 
1. Classify investments as adopters or casualties rather than position off 
averages.  
• Under a normal distribution, errors are symmetric and partially 
cancel each other out. But under bimodality they do not: to put a firm 
in one category or another means getting it completely right or 
wrong. Position size should track confidence in the classification. 
2. Avoid reading calm price action as low risk.  
• Trailing volatility measures dispersion that has already been realised. 
Bimodal separation is a jump, where the two modes look identical 
right until the moment when they don’t. A classic example is Chegg, 
an educational software maker which traded like a normal small-cap 
stock until one day in May 2023 when it halved on a single day after 
management linked slowing growth in new customers to ChatGPT. 
3. Averaging down, ie  scooping up shares whose price has fallen, may no 
longer be prudent. 
2. Can we get there theoretically? 
2.1 Scaling laws 
Scaling laws have provided the blueprint for improving AI over the past decade. 
It was a relationship between the amount of data, the amount of compute and 
the complexity of an AI model somewhat predictably informed how powerful a 
model would become. Google DeepMind’s Chinchilla model in 2022 showed that 
you could compensate for having a smaller model by using substantially more 
data. 
Scaling is older than the chatbot. In 1950 Claude Shannon built Theseus, a 
mechanical mouse that learned its way through a maze by trial and error. Epoch 
AI reckons it took about 40 operations of compute, known as FLOPs, to train. Grok 
4, trained in 2025, took roughly ten trillion trillion times as much. Across those 25 
orders of magnitude, more compute has brought more capability.

• A falling price may be information that a business model is obsolete 
rather than an opportunity.

### Page 9

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 9

Figure 5: Computation used to train notable AI systems (petaFLOP): log scale

Source: Epoch AI (citing estimates from AI literature), Our World in Data, Deutsche Bank Research

It is the robustness of this relationship that has made larger investments in 
compute capex a sine qua non for AI companies. 
However, this is not a law of physics and nothing guarantees it will continue . 
Indeed, in the ChatGPT era, five different approaches to scaling have been 
applied. Older strategies yield worse results, and new techniques, such as post -
training refinements, have been developed. The consensus amongst American 
and Chinese AI labs is that these will hold.  
While this may be a consensus amongst the commercial labs, there is scientific 
disagreement that apply ing scaling laws to today’s large -language models can 
achieve superintelligence. 
Yann LeCun, a Turing Award winner and formerly Meta's chief AI scientist, is blunt: 
"LLMs are not a path to superintelligence or even human-level intelligence”. Even 
inside the labs, the recipe is contested. Demis Hassabis of Google DeepMind has 
said he expects "one or two more big breakthroughs" to be needed "in addition to 
scaling". Ilya Sutskever, a co -founder and former chief scientist of OpenAI, has 
said the field is going "back to the age of research again, just with big computers". 
This is a scientific argument we are not in a position to evaluate , but for the 
purposes of our scenario, we will side with the labs’ position. 
For the moment, there is strong evidence that capabilities have in fact 
accelerated over the past three years, as reasoning models have outpaced non -
reasoning models by two or three times, according to Epoch data.

### Page 10

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 10

2.2 Recursive self-improvement: AI improving AI 
A cocktail-party definition of recursive self-improvement (RSI) is AI that improves 
itself. The reality is that it can be used in two ways: 
1. Self-improving agents that get better at using themselves: showing 
smarter use of prompts, tools, memory and workflows. The model -
building process is untouched. 
2. True RSI improves the process that builds the models: designing 
experiments, curating data, optimising algorithms and designing chips in 
a compounding loop. 
The former is like a factory worker who gets better at the job by learning the quirks 
of the machinery, getting organised and making fewer mistakes . The latter is like 
the worker redesigning the machine tools and then using those to build the next 
generation of machines. 
True RSI is what OpenAI’s chief scientist Jacob Pachoki referred to last month in 
a blog post about  “An Alien Mind ”. “Automated AI research is a more dramatic 
form of scaling intelligence with compute,” he wrote. “And of course as a part of 
it, AI will improve the computational substrate itself,” he added. Pachoki 
represents a broadly held consensus view amongst America n and Chinese labs 
that RSI is both possible and necessary for AI development. 
• OpenAI (June 2026): “AI doing AI research will become the determining 
factor of the pace of progress within the next few years.” 
• Google DeepMind’s Demis Hassabis (May 2026): “All the leading labs are 
quite focused on [RSI]…” Humanity is standing in the “foothills of the 
singularity”. 
• Alibaba CEO Eddie Wu (Sept 2026): RSI is “a concrete path forward,” with 
models autonomously designing experiments and driving a cycle of self-
evolution. 
For now, self -improving agents are the AI du jour. Noisy claims about the vast 
majority of coding being done by agents do not always stand up in the light of day 
from companies still hiring human coders at breakneck pace, albeit no longer to 
do the most mundane parts of the job.  
A better metric may be METR’s task -completion time horizon. This is measure of 
the complexity of software tasks that an AI agent can complete on its own, using 
the baseline of how long it would take a human expert to finish the task, at a 50 
percent success rate. So far the most advanced models have reached about 16 
hours, up from OpenAI’s GPT-4’s four minutes in March 2023.

### Page 11

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 11

Figure 6: Task horizon of software tasks that leading frontier models can complete 50% of the time (log scale)

Source: METR, Deutsche Bank Research

But software engineers can hold off on updating their resumes a little longer: 
there is a long road between 50 percent accuracy and the 99 percent or 100 
percent needed for serious enterprise use at scale, with the horizon potentially 
getting further away the nearer you get. Also METR notes that measurements 
above 16 hours are unreliable with the current task suite. As is the case across AI, 
the instrument is running out before the question is settled.  
More broadly, there are significant limitations to RSI:  
1. Research is clock-bound.  
• A frontier training run can take weeks on thousands of specialised chips, 
and researchers often cannot design the next experiment until the 
previous one has been completed.  
• That means that the speed of the loop is set by experiments completed 
per hour or day, not by ideas generated.  
• Compute is a hard ceiling that needs to be bought, buil t and connected 
to a grid. 
2. The real world defies verifiability.  
• The loop compounds only when the system can check its own output 
cheaply and automatically. 
• It is no coincidence that AI loves maths and coding. It is much harder to 
verify outputs over a long time horizon in the real world: does this drug 
work or does this strategy succeed?  
• RSI compounds fastest where the economy cares least and slows where 
the returns are largest.

### Page 12

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 12

3. Sequences are consequential.  
• Amdahl’s law finds that speed -up is capped when processes run in a 
sequence.  
• If you automate 90 percent, the residual 10 percent still limits you to a 
ten-times faster process. Nine women cannot produce a baby in one 
month.  
• The Usain Bolt of AI still has to run alongside the comparative tortoises 
of the training run itself, chip fabrication and real -world validation. Ten 
times faster is still not a regime break. 
This is borne out by METR’s series. Doubling every seven months is fast 
exponential growth but a take -off would show the doubling time itself – the 
second derivative – shrinking. Horizons are not (yet) exploding. 
Philosopher Toby Ord shows that recursive self -improvement can only reach a 
singularity if each cycle of improvement takes less time than the last, shrinking 
fast towards zero. He argues that real feedback loops have a floor: chips take time 
to make and models take time to train. A I progress could still outrun an 
exponential, but it would not go vertical. 
2.3 Scaling plus RSI: a match made in the race to AGI? 
No scaling and RSI means no short timeline for AGI. Without scaling, more 
compute buys nothing and capital is wasted. Without RSI, AI capability will 
continue to improve at the pace of flesh -and-blood human researchers, 
augmented by AI tools but not replac ed. Only with both will it be possible to 
produce a system that improves faster than the humans building it. Without one 
or the other, no quantity of chips, energy or capital will fix it. 
3. Can we get there practically? 
3.1 Infrastructure, chips and energy 
There is no consensus on the amount of training required to reach AGI. Estimates 
suggest that training 2026's top models required between 10^26 and 10^27 
FLOPs. If we hypothesise that an "AGI -like model" would require three orders of 
magnitude more training, this would require 10^30 FLOPs.   
Consider today’s US AI infrastructure, which runs to approximately 10^22 FLOPs. 
If all of this was conceivably used for a 10^30 FLOP training run, it would take 
nearly four years to complete.  
The physical infrastructure to practically support such a training run would 
require enormous power, perhaps around 6GW, about 50% more than the four 
Vogtle nuclear power plants recently activated in the US. The cost of the 
compute infrastructure would run to between $300bn and $500bn.  
Today’s advanced chips would simply not suffice. Back-of-the-envelope maths 
suggests that even using Nvidia’s most advanced Vera Rubin chips, training such 
a model in four months would require 7.8m chips and 26GW of facility power. 
That would cost $900bn today and a rental equivalent cost of $90bn.  
Of course, frontier compute is growing rapidly, and price -performance is 
declining. If current trends hold, a 2030 -era cluster capable of such a run would

### Page 13

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 13

need perhaps $200bn in computer and power hardware for a training run costing 
about $20bn.

Figure 7: Technology cost index over time (years since start of price decline)

Source: Epoch AI, Deutsche Bank Research

The hyperscalers are leading the charge towards AGI. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, 
Microsoft and Oracle are set to invest almost $800bn in capital expenditure this 
year, mostly for ever more powerful data centres, and over $1 trillion a year 
through 2030 and beyond.

Figure 8: Hyperscaler capital expenditure (including estimates)

Source: Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research

### Page 14

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 14

By 2030, assuming no market retrenchment, this means additional $4 trillion to 
$6 trillion on AI compute capability invested. This infrastructure could 
accommodate a training run of 10^30 FLOPs with some comfort.  
But even if AI labs crack the secret sauce of RSI, and their finance chiefs are willing 
to invest in such large training runs, and hyperscalers are prepared to continue to 
build the infrastructure, the infrastructure needs to be built.  
Grid connection queues, turbine lead times and transmission build are measured 
in years, not the months it takes to assemble and air-freight the latest Nvidia chip. 
The lead time for substation transformers is currently as long as three years. 
Politics in the US has shifted towards slowing the pace of the AI build out and this 
force will run against the demands for larger data centres that would be essential 
for RSI.  
3.2 Capital 
Financing for AI has been abundant in the historic buildout of the past three years. 
However, it is still largely conditional not on revenue but on belief in a future 
payoff. 
RSI is a “key part of the investment thesis” in the “biggest scientific bet civilisation 
has ever made,” Google DeepMind’s chief strategy officer Jasjeet Sekhon said at 
a conference in August, The Information reported. “We have this danger we could 
hit an AI air pocket such that the expenditures happen but the revenues don’t 
show up.” 
An inflection point is approaching where the nature of investment changes from 
free cash flow and equity to debt, with all that entails in terms of exposure to 
rising rates and creditor appetite. The hyperscalers are reaching a point where 
capex is no long er covered by operating cashflow and where net debt exceeds 
earnings before interest, depreciation and amortisation (EBITDA). 
Alphabet, for example, posted its first quarterly free  cash outflow in nearly two 
decades in its most recent quarter, even as it lifted its full-year capex guidance to 
$205bn. It said it would continue to rent third -party capacity and reported a 
backlog of $514bn in contracted, not-yet-recognised data centre revenue. 
3.3 Data 
Data has been the new oil for some time now. But unfortunately for AI 
development it seems to be running out even faster. 
The effective stock of quality - and repetition -adjusted public human text was 
about 300 trillion tokens, with a vast 90 percent confidence interval of 100 trillion 
to 1,000 trillion, Epoch estimated in 2024. A token is roughly equivalent to three 
quarters of a word. By that count it was likely to be fully used between 2026 and 
2032.

### Page 15

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 15

Figure 9: Projections of stock of public text and data usage

Source: Epoch AI, Deutsche Bank Research

However, since then, rather than data dropping off a cliff, there has been a rise in 
synthetic data generation, transfer learning from data-rich domains such as video 
and telemetry, and data efficiency. While the data shortage has not been 
resolved, the immediate crisis has been averted. 
AI now makes much of its own training data. When DeepMind's AlphaGo became 
superhuman at playing the mind-bendingly complex board game Go, it did so not 
by playing human experts, but by playing millions and millions of games against 
itself. 
For many domains relating to coding and mathematical proofs where automatic 
checks are possible, such synthetic experience can move things forward.  That is 
the case with AI research, where the data may be the results of experiments, all 
of which are bought by compute. About 90% of OpenAI's 2024 research compute 
went on such experiments. 
And increasingly, people write the tests rather than the answers. By the summer 
of 2026, selling training data for practi ce tasks was a nearly $9bn revenue 
business. Firms such as Mercor hire experts to generate long tasks against which 
the AI can be trained. 
The challenge here is that trust in the checker becomes a scarce input.

### Page 16

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 16

As AI makes production cheaper, it also makes establishing that production is 
accurate or reliable more expensive.  Domains which can be checked 
mechanically, such as mathematics, will be opened up more quickly. But those 
which rely on expert verification, such as medicine, engineering or law, may 
progress more slowly. 
The likely implications are twofold: a slower rate of diffusion into economic 
domains that are less verifiable and a larger share of production costs devoted to 
assurance. 
Another issue is that s ystems that have to  grade themselves learn to game the 
process. T his was partially what happened when an OpenAI model escap ed a 
sandbox and hacked into a third-party website. Trust replaced data as a wall. 
There are also question marks over the quality of data that is recycled repeatedly 
through the system. Although humans continue to produce original content, half 
of internet articles are now primarily generated by AI, Graphite data show.

Figure 10: Human vs primarily AI-generated articles on the internet

Source: Graphite (citing 55.4k articles from Common Crawl examined by AI detectors), Deutsche Bank Research

3.4 An incomplete case 
This analysis shows that the San Francisco Consensus, which emphasises RSI and 
a short timeline to AGI, is neither predestined nor impossible.  
Rather it depends on whether the science is right, on whether scaling is all you 
need, and on whether the infrastructure and capital required for that scaling is 
deployed and accessible to the leading labs.  
There is, in other words, a possible rather than impossible, path to AGI, subject to 
those conditions.  
None of this proves that superintelligence will ever arrive. A buildup at this scale 
has few peacetime precedents and vastly exceeds the likes of the Manhattan 
Project and Apollo programme.

### Page 17

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 17

It is uncertain whether there will be enough demand to pay upfront for the 
investment in achieving RSI that would justify that investment. And whether the 
friction of verification, and inevitable declining marginal returns, act as a drag on 
what otherwise might be a runaway process. 
4. What happens if we don’t get there? The base case 
4.1 Separating AI progress from superintelligence 
So what if that thesis is wrong? Does the AI trade depend on a journey to AGI?  
AI can remain economically important even before RSI coupled with scaling laws 
lead to super intelligence . Companies do not need to wait for nebulous 
superintelligence to generate identifiable returns from AI in the medium term as 
they fold it into their systems, streamline workflows and roll out new business 
models. 
Investors already have exposure to RSI as a capital allocation because the leading 
US labs say RSI is a key part of their product strategy. And it is these labs whose 
products are catalysing and amplifying the infrastructure boom. 
Even r apid competitive diffusion does not mean every part of the physical 
economy changes instantly. 
The smartest superintelligence might identify the optimal buyout structure 
before breakfast, but it cannot force a financially constrained buyer to use it or 
persuade the seller to accept less than they have been expecting. Those 
challenges will be even more intractable where the parties are more complex, say, 
fiscally constrained sovereigns or foreign equity holders. 
4.2 Why the base case is attractive 
Superintelligence would be a chaotic accelerant to human society, and a 
potential regime break. But it is not the only scenario in which today’s investment 
in AI infrastructure makes economic sense. 
Adoption of AI tools by businesses is progressing approximately three times faster 
than the mobile, internet and cloud computing waves. From sector revenues 
below $1bn in 2022, the AI economy, excluding China, will likely exceed $200bn 
in revenues in 2026.  
Early winners are the cloud businesses. Google Cloud revenue jumped 82 percent 
from a year earlier in the most recent quarter as companies turned AI pilots into 
workflows. Amazon Web Services sales were up 37 percent and Microsoft’s AI 
business more than doubled. 
There is also slow, but increasing, evidence of a productivity uplift. The J-curve 
cuts both ways: front -loading investments is taking one step back to take two 
steps forward. The so -called complementary intangible investment that is 
(mis)measured as cost today to the detriment of the productivity statistics will 
flatter them in the years to come as it pays off. The base case does not require a 
regime break to show up as a productivity surprise, just for the accounting to 
catch up. 
The economic gains from AI that is less powerful than the AGI mooted by the labs 
would be substantial. And in this base case, they may also be attractive.

### Page 18

12 October 2026 
Thematic Research

Deutsche Bank AG Page 18

Hyperscaler capex is driving earnings at beneficiaries ranging from 
semiconductor makers to construction companies and accounting for between 
one-third and one -half of current US growth. The wealth effect of rising stock 
prices, with  the ratio of  US equity wealth to income rising from around 1.8 to 
almost 2.5 over the past three years, is supporting consumer spending. 
AI FOMO (fear of missing out) is buoying enterprise spending on cloud services 
and the groundwork for AI initiatives. Those are beginning to bear fruit in terms of 
personal productivity, cost savings and more targeted products and services. 
4.3 Investor watchpoints 
The arrival of ASI would be a disruptive economic shock. Investors need to be alert 
for the early signals. 
Rather than relying on a single probability forecast, investors should watch five 
indicators particularly closely for signs that AGI is nearing.  
1. AI speeds up AI 
• Track METR’s estimate of how much AI speeds up AI. Currently the 
capabilities of models are doubling roughly every three months. In a 
world of RSI, the pace of this speed-up will itself likely accelerate. 
2. AI can complete whole jobs 
• Track via the Remote Labor Index, a multi-sector benchmark. Currently it 
finds that the highest performing agent still only manages to automate 
20 percent of the real-world, economically valuable freelance projects it 
attempts. 
3. Compute remains scarce 
• On a path to superintelligence, demand for compute will continue to 
outrun supply. Track GPU one-year rental prices, up by about a third over 
the past year. 
4. Firms bifurcate 
• Leaders and laggards in the same industry diverge while averages stay 
the same. Look at revenue per employee in top - and bottom -quartile 
firms 
5. Junior hiring comes under significant pressure 
• … And then the pressure rises up the ranks.

giảm. Linh kiện ô tô, Dầu khí và Công nghệ là các nhóm ngành có m ức giảm rõ rệt nhất 
trong tuần. Khối ngoại duy trì bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị -634 tỷ 
đồng, tập trung ở các mã FPT, ACB và VPL; mặt khác, tự doanh đảo chiều bán ròng ở mức 
-609 tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại đảo chiều bán ròng -5,829 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần 
qua và đang duy trì vị thế bán lũy kế -1,462 hợp đồng. Tự doanh duy trì mua ròng 722 hợp 
đồng nhưng đang duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -5,278 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam đánh mất đà hồi phục của tuần trước, khi VN-Index quay đầu giảm khoảng 
1,8% về đóng cửa quanh 1.742 điểm, với phiên cuối tuần có thời điểm lùi sâu về sát 1.727 
điểm trước khi thu hẹp đà giảm. Dù mùa báo cáo KQKD quý 3 diễn ra tương đối tích cực, 
tuy nhiên các lo ngại về việc môi trường lãi suất cao vẫn duy trì trong bối cảnh căng thẳng 
Trung Đông leo thang đang đè nặng lên tâm lý nhà đầu tư. Kết quả kỳ họp của FED trong 
tuần qua nhìn chung phù hợp với kỳ vọng và không gây ra ảnh hưởng đáng kể. Điểm kém 
tích cực nhất là khối ngoại đã quay lại bán ròng với tổng giá trị khoảng 2.700 tỷ đồng 
trong tuần — đảo ngược diễn biến mua ròng cuối tuần trước, cho thấy đà rút vốn ngoại 
vẫn chưa thực sự dừng lại.

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX tuần qua hình thành mẫu nến giảm trên đồ thị tuần, nhưng chưa thoái lui quá 1 
nửa cây nến tăng của tuần trước, cùng thanh khoản được tiết giảm, hàm ý nhịp điều chỉnh 
vẫn trong phạm vi cho phép. Trên khung ngày, sau bốn phiên giằng co đầu tuần với thanh 
khoản thấp, chỉ số đã chịu áp lực sụt giảm mạnh hơn trong phiên cuối tuần, chớm xuống 
dưới đường tín hiệu chính giữa dải Bollinger, nhưng cho tín hiệu rút chân ngay sau đó, thể 
hiện áp lực bán tháo đã tạm thời được kiểm soát. Mặc dù rủi ro mở rộng thêm nhịp điều 
chỉnh vẫn còn để ngỏ, tuy nhiên khi xu hướng tăng ngắn hạn đang được bảo lưu, VNINDEX 
được kỳ vọng sẽ cho phản ứng hồi phục trở lại tại các điểm đỡ bên dưới.

(Ghi chú: NĐT có thể dựa theo nhận định xu hướng thị trường để tham khảo khuyến nghị 
hành động tương ứng trong bảng phía trên)

Danh mục đầu tư mẫu

Phương pháp tiếp cận: 
- Danh mục tập hợp các cổ phiếu tiềm năng nhất được KBSV 
lựa chọn và tái cơ cấu theo tháng. 
- Ngưỡng cắt lỗ cần được cân nhắc cho mỗi mã là -15%. 
- Danh mục giả định luôn duy trì tỷ trọng 100% là cổ phiếu với 
trọng số được phân bổ đều giữa các mã.

Lưu ý:  
- NĐT được khuyến nghị mua tại mức giá bằng hoặc thấp hơn 
giá khuyến nghị mua. 
- Lợi nhuận kỳ vọng được tính theo giá khuyến nghị mua. 
 So sánh hiệu suất với VN30 Index

Earnings above all 
• Higher oil prices are in a tussle with stronger growth. We expect strong growth 
to help the global economy weather inflation risks, allowing equities to focus on the 
strength of earnings growth. Oil prices remain the key risk to monitor 
• Maintain a preference for equities over bonds and cash.  Strong earnings 
growth means we would use the recent pause to add exposure to our preferred 
markets and themes. The US and Asia ex-Japan remain our preferred regions 
• Take advantage of higher bond yields via credit risk but cap duration. The 
risk of higher bond yields looks most pressing in ultra -long maturities, but credit 
quality remains strong. We are Overweight EM USD bonds 
Hits from the 1990s 
Rising bond yields have been a key market theme.  Since mid -year, 10 -year 
government bond yields have risen approximately 40-80bps across the US, Japan and 
Euro area. Against this backdrop, gold has delivered high single -digit returns, while 
global equities have delivered low single-digit returns. 
Higher oil prices are in a tussle with robust growth data . The lack of progress 
towards a negotiated settlement in the US -Iran conflict and rising fears of shipping 
restrictions extending to the Bab al-Mandab Strait have meant that oil prices are once 
again testing the USD 100/bbl level. These worries have been exacerbated by 
increasing worries about refined product (eg. diesel, jet fuel, etc) supply. Despite these 
concerns, US growth data has held up remarkably well, as illustrated by the latest PMI 
and retail sales data. This is underpinned by AI investment growth.  
Real, rather than nominal, energy prices are key.  A chart of inflation -adjusted 
energy prices, though, illustrates why the global economy has managed to hold up 
despite these risks. Oil prices in real (inflation-adjusted) terms are closer to long term 
averages rather than at true ‘oil shock’ levels. This, together with the global economy’s 
lower oil intensity relative to history, is why we expect  growth and inflation to avoid 
worst-case scenarios as long as nominal oil prices avoid an excursion into the USD 
120-150/bbl range.

1 Investment strategy and key themes

Steve Brice 
Global Chief Investment Officer 
Manpreet Gill 
Chief Investment Officer, EMEA 
Raymond Cheng, CFA, CPA 
Chief Investment Officer, North Asia

The Fed is likely to hike twice more by mid -2027. The 
acceleration in AI-driven investment growth means we believe 
the chances of a shift from our core soft-landing to no-landing 
economic scenario have gone up. This, combined with still -
high inflation readings mean we now expect the Fed to hike 
rates twice more by mid -2027. In contrast, the BoJ and ECB 
are expected to hike thrice and once more respectively. 
Buy-the-dip in equities 
1990s rate hikes a better parallel then 2022 . There is 
understandably some concern about whether rising central 
bank policy rates, and rising bond yields across maturities, will 
present a challenge to equity markets. However, we believe 
rate hikes in the 1990s are a better historical parallel, when 
central banks were hiking rates into strong economic and 
earnings growth, resulting in equity markets maintaining their 
pace of gains. The 2022 hiking cycle, in contrast, represented 
an inflation surprise, which we believe is not the case today. 
Strong earnings growth overwhelms other factors today. 
Still-strong economic and earnings growth means that we 
remain constructive on the outlook for equities and other risky 
assets going into year -end and beyond. The pause over the 
last couple of months was likely driven by a combination or 
rising bond yields , September seasonality and excessive 
investor optimism around mid-year.  
However, earnings expectations for global equities remain 
very strong (especially in our preferred US and Asia ex-Japan 
regional markets). We believe these earnings remain 
sufficiently robust to outweigh the drag from higher bond 
yields. Therefore, w e remain Overweight equities relative to 
bonds and cash, though we believe it is prudent to ensure the 
size of the equities Overweight is not excessive. Within Asia, 
we also trim China equities to a core holding, leaving Taiwan 
as our only regional Overwei ght, given the ongoing focus on 
AI investment-driven markets for now. 
Bumps remain part of the journey. As we laid out in our mid-
year outlook, the second half of this year is expected to be 
characterised by positive, but bumpy returns as a result of the 
factors laid out in the previous section, but also US mid -term 
election risks. The second half has thus far played out as 
expected given sufficient bumps, but still robust earnings 
growth. We would not be surprised by some volatility around 
US mid-term elections, but any further pullbacks or pauses 
would be opportunit ies to raise exposure given strong 
earnings growth is expected to ultimately dominate. 
Prefer credit over duration 
Corporate credit quality remains strong . Aggregate 
indicators of credit quality suggest credit quality remains 
strong across both Investment Grade and High Yield bonds 
across most regions. US IG bond yields face some upward 
pressure from significant hyperscaler supply, but supply -
driven spread widening tends to be relatively short -lived in 
nature and should not signal significant credit quality worries. 
Longer-maturity bonds remain at risk of further volatility. 
In contrast, we continue to be concerned that the volatility in 
very long maturity bonds could extend. Continued inflation 
and debt worries mean it is possible investors continue to 
demand a greater ‘term premium’ (ie. a premium for holding 
bonds with very  long maturities). We continue to believe 
limiting bond duration to 3-7 years remains appropriate.  
We continue to prefer  corporate and EM over government 
bonds. This implies earning a higher yield by taking on more 
credit risk (via corporate or EM bonds) rather than adding 
more duration risk. Within bonds, we remain Overweight EM 
USD bonds and view IG and HY bonds as core holdings. 
Modest USD weakness; gold to grind higher 
USD bounce led by pricing of Fed hikes . However, we 
believe Fed hikes are now excessively priced. At the same 
time, we see room for non-US rates to rise further, especially 
in Japan where the undervalued JPY is likely to strengthen. 
This is why modest USD weakness is likely to resume. 
Gold to continue grinding higher. While the move higher is 
likely to be characteri sed by two -steps-forward-one-step-
back, we believe an ultimately softer USD and continued EM 
central bank demand should result in continued gains in the 
precious metal over both the next 3- and 12-month periods.

### Page 7

Global Market Outlook 7

Summary

With the Federal Reserve’s latest decision to raise its benchmark rate by 25 basis points in September 2026, as well as the one 
to two additional hikes predicted  before the end of the year, we are entering a new interest -rate hiking cycle. This marks an 
important shift for investors, but higher policy rates do not necessarily imply weaker portfolio returns. What matters is why rates 
are rising, how quickly they increase, and whether economic growth can absorb tighter financial conditions. 
Our framework places the current environment in a healthy/high-yield regime. Growth remains resilient, credit spreads are 
tight and the yield curve is normalising. At the same time, the economy is approaching an important boundary. Inflation remains 
above target and the 10-year US government bond yield has reached 5%. A renewed rise in inflation or a sharper slowdown in 
activity could move markets into an unhealthy/high-yield regime. 
This argues for  measured risk-taking rather than an aggressively defensive stance . We maintain a slight overweight in 
equities, hold a strong overweight in gold and a neutral allocation to fixed income. Diversification remains key near a regim e 
boundary, and hedge funds can provide useful sources of return that are less dependent on the direction of traditional markets.

Not all interest-rate hiking cycles are the same

Traditional market 
analysis often treats 
rising rates as uniformly 
negative. History 
suggests a more 
nuanced conclusion.  
We classify hiking 
cycles along two 
dimensions: whether  
the increase in rates is 
healthy or unhealthy, 
and whether it occurs  
in a high- or low-yield 
environment. 
 Fig. 8 The different regimes of interest-rate hiking cycle 
Historical interest-rate hiking cycle defined by real growth and US 10-Year treasury yields

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered; Since 1974

A healthy increase in rates is generally associated with firm 
demand, above -trend growth, resilient equities and stable 
credit markets. Central banks can move gradually and remain 
data-dependent because the economy is strong enough to 
withstand tighter fin ancial conditions. An unhealthy increase 
is more likely to be driven by supply constraints or persistent 
inflationary pressures. Growth slows, credit spreads widen, 
equities de-rate and central banks are forced to react more 
aggressively. 
The second dimension is the starting level of yields. In a high-
yield environment, bonds offer meaningful income and a 
greater cushion against moderate price declines. In a low -
yield environment, that protection is limited. This distinction 
matters because the same increase in yields can have very 
different portfolio consequences depending on the income 
available at the outset.

Assessing today’s market regime: Walking the 
fine line  
We assess the current environment as healthy/high yield 
(see Fig. 9), although it is moving closer to the boundary with 
an unhealthy/high-yield regime. Growth remains resilient, with 
2026 consensus GDP growth of around 2.1%, but it is below 
its longer-term trend. Inflation has eased , but remains above 
target, with headline CPI at 3.4% year -on-year in August 
2026, leaving the Fed focused on the balance between 
persistent price pressures and a potential growth slowdown. 
Market signals over the next 6-12 months remain broadly 
supportive. The 10-year US government bond yield is around 
5%, providing the highest bond carry since 2007, while the 
yield curve is re -steepening after its prolonged inversion.  
Equities remain positive, supported by AI -related earnings 
and soft-landing expectations, and high -yield credit spreads 
are near post-global-financial-crisis lows. 
Overall, resilient markets and benign credit conditions support 
the healthy classification. However, slower growth and above-
target inflation leave little room for disappointment. Further 
disinflation would help sustain the current regime, while sticky 
inflation, a rapid rise in yields or a sharp growth slowdown 
could push it into unhealthy territory.  
Higher rates, different outcomes 
Historical returns vary significantly across the four regimes 
(see Fig. 10), but history shows that some hiking regimes 
have generated solid returns across major asset classes. 
Unhealthy regimes are more complicated. Unhealthy/high -
yield periods (top-left corner) have historically still generated 
positive average returns, but these averages can conceal 
considerable volatility and dispersion. The most damaging 
combination has been unhealthy/low yield (as in 2022), when 
traditional 60/40 portfolios declined by an average of 15.8%. 
The lesson is that the economic cause of higher rates, and the 
level of yields, matter more than the label "rate hike" alone.

Average returns for equities, bonds, Gold and a 60% 
equity/40% bond portfolio during rate-hiking periods.

World and global aggregate benchmarks, Standard Chartered 
Why equities can coexist with 5% bond yields 
The absolute level of bond yields does not, by itself, determine 
equity performance. Investors must also consider the 
direction, speed and underlying cause of the move. 
When yields rise gradually because growth and 
investment demand are strong, corporate earnings 
growth can offset pressure on valuation multiples.  By 
contrast, a rapid increase driven by an inflation shock or 
disorderly repricing is more difficult for equities to absorb. 
Historically, several high -yield episodes combined positive 
real growth with resilient equity markets, while inflation-driven 
episodes, such as in 2022, were more challenging. 
The current level of yields has also been substantially 
incorporated into market prices. Investors have had time to 
adjust discount rates, equity valuations and financing 
assumptions to a world in which the 10-year Treasury yield is 
around 5%. This does not eliminate risk, but it means high 
yields are no longer a wholly new shock. The greater  threat 
would be a further rapid and unexpected increase in yields. 
AI investment sits at the centre of today’s market dynamic. 
Heavy spending on data centres, semiconductors, power 
generation and digital infrastructure is supporting earnings 
and helping equities remain resilient, despite elevated yields. 
At the same time, AI-related capital demand is competing with 
rising government borrowing and bond issuance. If 
productivity gains and earnings growth ultimately justify the 
investment, higher yields and stronger equities can coex ist.

### Page 9

Global Market Outlook 9

However, if bond supply outpaces available savings or AI 
returns disappoint, yields could rise more sharply and put 
pressure on valuations. The balance between productive 
investment demand and expanding sovereign debt supply 
remains a key market theme. 
Translating the hiking-cycle regimes and 
yields level into portfolio positioning 
Our positioning reflects a healthy/high -yield regime, while 
recognising we are close to the boundary with a less 
favourable environment. Historically, this regime has 
supported positive returns across equities, bonds, gold and 
60/40 portfolios. However, pe rformance has varied 
considerably depending on whether yields were rising, stable 
or falling (see Fig. 11), reinforcing the case for selective risk -
taking and broad diversification. 
Fig. 11 Asset average returns under different high-yield 
scenarios 
Historical average performance of major asset classes 
across different path of bond yields.

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
We maintain a slight overweight in equities, focused on US 
AI-related stocks and Asia ex -Japan. Equities performed 
particularly well when high yields were stable, historically 
returning 15.1% on average, but struggled when yields rose. 
Our moderate overweight , therefore, allows participation in 
resilient growth and earnings while limiting exposure to a 
renewed rate shock. 
We hold a strong overweight in gold, which performed well 
in both rising- and stable-yield environments and delivered the 
highest return of any asset when high yields were stable. Our 
fixed-income allocation is neutral . Although today’s yields 
offer attractive income, bonds have historically been 
vulnerable when yields were rising and underperformed when 
yield were stable. 
Diversification beyond a traditional 60/40 portfolio 
remains important  at this inflection point. Hedge funds 
generated positive returns across all regimes  - rising, stable 
and falling high -yield periods  - making them a useful 
complement when the direction of rates and the path between 
healthy and unhealthy regimes is uncertain.

### Page 10

Global Market Outlook 10

Key themes

Core scenario (soft landing, 45% probability): We have slightly reduced the probability of a soft landing from 50% by raising 
the odds of a ‘no -landing’. The global economy has  picked up steam this summer, powered by AI investments in the US and 
Asia. This is reflected in robust upgrades to corporate earnings estimates  and PMIs. Fiscal easing in Germany and Japan is 
supporting growth. Meanwhile, inflation has picked up amid higher energy prices due to a resurgen t Middle East conflict. As a 
result, the Fed turned hawkish, hiking rates for the first time since 2023. It was joined by the ECB and BoJ – the first time all 
three major central banks hiked rates in the same month. We expect the Fed to hike twice more, the ECB once more and the 
BoJ thrice more by June 2027. Nevertheless, disinflation should return by next year as energy and tariff effects fade, enabling 
the Fed and the ECB to cut rates by Q3 2027. Meanwhile, China is likely to accelerate fiscal spending to revive domestic growth.  
Upside risk (no landing, 30% probability): We raised the probability from 25% amid an acceleration in the AI-driven investment 
growth. If oil prices ease, US tax cuts, an AI -fuelled stock market boom and fiscal easing in Germany and Japan could boost 
“animal spirits” further. A global defence spending boom, led by the US and Europe, could also spur global growth. 
Downside risk (25% probability): This tail risk scenario includes 15% chance of a stagflation unfolding if geopolitical conflicts 
worsen and oil stays above USD 120/bbl. We also assign a 10% chance to a recession, potentially caused by a more rapid pace 
of rate hikes to quell inflation, a stock market downturn negating the wealth effect, or a bond selloff due to fiscal concerns.

Key chart

deliver another insurance

once a quarter until June

 Fig. 12 More rate hikes until June 2027, before cuts in H2 2027 as inflation pressures fade 
Our policy rate outlook*; oil price and US, Euro area, German near-term, long-term inflation

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered; *dotted lines reflect our expected rates path over the next 12m

Policy rates watch  
Fed turns hawkish to build credibility as oil reignites 
inflation; likely to hike twice more by June 2027. The US 
economy appears resilient, with consensus estimates for 
2026 GDP clustering around 2.1% for both 2026 and 2027. 
The main engine is the AI investment boom and hyperscaler 
datacentre buildout, reinforced by easy financial conditions 
and healthy private-sector balance sheets. The robust outlook 
was reflected in August’s PMI data, with both manufacturing 
and service sector confidence beating expectations. 
The labour market has picked up  this year , with average 
monthly payrolls of 80 ,000 vs. 10 ,000 in 2025 . The 
unemployment rate at 4.1%  suggests full employment, and 
the workweek lengthened in August. Offsetting vulnerabilities 
include a low savings rate, fading fiscal support, soft real  
personal spending and oil prices close to USD 100/bbl. 
Inflation remains a challenge. Core PCE inflation has run 
above 3% every month since March, and the hawkish case 
rests on supercore inflation remaining around 3 %, raising 
concerns about energy cost pass-through. Nevertheless, 
August's firm inflation print came from airfares, hotels and a 
record jump in wireless plans  (which is idiosyncratic) , while 
shelter cooled and core goods rose just 0. 7% y/y. Core 
consumer inflation at 2. 4% y/y is the lowest since 2021, and 
BEA methodology changes in end-September should 
mechanically shave 0.2-0.3pp off historical core PCE inflation. 
Following the unanimous Fed vote to hike in September, and 
still elevated inflation, we pencil in one more rate hike this 
year, followed by another 25bps hike to 4.5% by June 2027. 
This pace is slower than market pricing of another c. 90bps of 
hikes in the next 12 months. However, we expect disinflation 
to resume next year as the impact of oil prices and tariffs fade. 
This should create space for the Fed to cut rates by Q3 2027.

6 Macro overview – at a glance

Rajat Bhattacharya

### Page 11

Global Market Outlook 11 
ECB to hike once more: Euro area activity has proved more 
resilient than expected. The consensus 2026 growth estimate 
has been revised up to 0.9%, from 0.5% as recently as July, 
with ECB staff projections upgraded to  1.4% for 2027 and 
1.5% for 2028. Manufacturing PMIs have improved to 52.7 
regionally and services PMI to 53.0, helped by German fiscal 
delivery and falling household savings rates. A softer Q3 looks 
weather-driven, due to heatwaves and low Rhine water levels, 
though higher energy prices and real yields still cap demand. 
Headline inflation hit a near three -year high of 3.3% y/y in 
August and may peak around 3.4 -3.6% in Q4 2026 before 
easing through 2027. Underlying pressures look calmer: core 
inflation drifted down to 2.4%, services eased, and negotiated 
wage growth slowed to 2.4% y/y in Q2, down from a peak of 
5.6% in 2024 . Long -term inflation expectations remain 
anchored with no evidence of second -round effects. Inflation 
risks are two -sided – oil above USD 100/bbl and gas above 
EUR 70/MWh are fuelling near -term inflation, which in turn 
should hurt demand, driving inflation below 2% by H2 2027. 
The ECB raised its deposit rate to 2.50% in September, the 
top of neutral estimates. We see scope for one more rate hike 
to 2.75% by year -end before a pause in H1 2027. As such 
pricing of 95bps more tightening toward an above-3% terminal 
rate appears too hawkish. We expect easing of energy-driven 
price pressures to allow for ECB rate cuts by Q3 2027. 
China to boost fiscal spending to support growth. China 
is entering a stabilisation phase in which production is firming, 
but the recovery remains narrow. The official manufacturing 
PMI rose to 49.8 in August from 49.2, with new orders, export 
orders and production back in expansionary territory, while 
export growth accelerated to 25.0% y/y. Momentum, however, 
is concentrated in high -tech and export manufacturing: retail 
sales, fixed-asset investment and credit growth remain soft, 
and the property downturn has yet to bottom. Full -year 2026 
growth should land near 4.5 –4.6%, broadly in line with the 
official 4.5–5% target, as domestic demand stays weak. 
Reflation is under way, but shallow and cost-driven rather than 
demand-led. Headline consumer inflation edged up to 0.8% 
y/y in August and producer inflation to 3.8%, lifted by higher 
commodity and energy prices and by official efforts to curb 
excessive price-cutting. Full-year inflation is expected around 
0.8–1.0%, against roughly zero in 2025, with producer 
inflation swinging from −2.6% to about +2.5%. The persistent 
supply-demand imbalance should cap the reflation impulse. 
Policy settings remain supportive, but with no rush to 
stimulate. Fiscal policy takes the primary role via faster 
spending and utilisation of bond proceeds, with broad fiscal 
spending potentially accelerating in H2 versus a sharp 
contraction in H1, and the augmented fiscal deficit widening 
modestly. Monetary policy plays a complementary role: a 
25bps cut in bank reserve requirements is likely, while the 
policy rate should stay unchanged. Slow public financing 
raises the likelihood of further policy fine-tuning this autumn. 
BoJ to hike once a quarter until Q2 2027. Japan's economy 
is proving more resilient than expected. The consensus 2026 
growth estimate has been revised up over the past month to 
0.8% from 0.6%. Exports rose 19% y/y in August, a twelfth 
consecutive y/y growth, while core machinery and machine 
tool orders indicate higher borrowing costs have yet to dent 
corporate investment appetite. Real wages climbed 2.4% y/y 
in July, the strongest since May 2021. The weak link is the 
household sector: spending continued to contract y/y, leaving 
an unusual two -speed economy in which external and 
corporate demand carry growth while domestic demand lags. 
Underlying inflation sits close to the 2% target, with pressure 
building upstream rather than from demand. Energy and 
commodity prices have risen sharply and earlier yen 
weakness has amplified import costs, with producer inflation 
running well ahead of CPI . The 10-year breakeven inflation 
has risen close to May’s record high of 2.3% amid growing risk 
of pass-through from producer to consumer prices.  
Bank of Japan Governor Ueda, after raising rates by 25bps to 
1.25% in September, alluded to rising inflation pressures, 
stating the policy environment has shifted to a “new phase”. 
He added that the BoJ must now act to prevent inflation from 
overshooting, rather than simply trying to reach the 2% target. 
September’s BoJ decision came in a 7-2 split vote, with two 
members nominated by Prime Minister Takaichi calling for a 
hold. However, Ueda’s post -decision comments lead us to 
believe that the BoJ board will be comfortable with a 25bps 
rate hike every quarter until Q2 2027, taking the rate to 2%.

### Page 12

Global Market Outlook 12

Our view

Foundation: Fixed income assets are a  core holding (Neutral) in our portfolios. Within the asset class, we maintain an 
Overweight stance on EM USD government bonds and an Underweight stance on DM government bonds.  
The Fed’s decision to hike rates in the past week confirms our expectations of a central bank willing to establish its independence 
against political pressure. The Fed's moderately hawkish pivot is helping restore its inflation-fighting credentials. We expect Fed 
to hike twice, one by year-end and another hike in H1 2027 to  bring the Fed funds rate to 4.5%. The price reaction in the US 
government bond yield curve le ads us to believe the short -to-middle part of the curve ha s factored in most of the rate 
expectations. We now prefer to add positions in the 3-7-year bond maturity bucket. As oil price moderates in H2 2027, we see 
room for the Fed to contemplate rate cuts. That would also enable 10-year yields to come down, alongside the front end of the 
curve. Meanwhile, solid corporate fundamentals and supportive technicals should keep yield premiums tight, even if the scope 
for further compression is limited. In EM, we continue to favour EM USD over local currency (LCY) government bonds. 
Opportunistic ideas: We are bullish on European bank additional tier-1 (AT1) bonds (contingent convertibles [CoCos1]; FX-
hedged), US TIPS, AAA-rated CLOs, US utilities corporate hybrids, broad US HY and AUD corporate bonds.

DM rates – Underweight 
We are Underweight DM government bonds. In the US, the 
Fed's policy pivot this month followed Chair Warsh's hawkish 
Jackson Hole speech and recent economic data prints, 
including a pick-up in job creation and inflation over the summer 
and a sharp rebound in oil prices due to the re-escalation of the 
Middle East conflict. Given the unanimous Fed decision to hike 
rates this week, we expect two more hikes by June 2027 on the 
back of robust growth amid strong AI investments and a 
balanced labour market, keeping inflation elevated.  
Over the next few months, we expect the 2-, 10- and 30-year 
US government bond yields to remain rangebound, with the 
10-year yield trading in the 5.00-5.25% range. The reasons 
for this include: 1) oil prices staying elevated and potentially 
rising further due to the fluid geopolitical situation in the Middle 
East; 2) AI capex plans showing no signs of slowing down, 
which continues to drive above -trend growth in the economy 
and AI -related heavy bond issuance. While short -covering 
trades could move bond yields lower temporarily as net-short 
positions in US government bond futures have reached 
extreme levels, we think such downshifts would be short-lived. 
By H2 2027, we expect oil prices to moderate and the 
inflationary effects from tariffs to fade, allowing the Fed to 
contemplate rate cuts. US 30 -year yields, on the other hand, 
are expected to remain elevated as the US fiscal deficit 
remains substantial next year. In addition, we do not think the 
so-called ‘Bessent put’  - expanding purchases of long -dated 
bonds, while skewing new issuance towards shorter 
maturities - will be effective as this does not address the 
fundamental issues  of rising debt levels . These factors are 
likely to keep the 'term premium' elevated. 
In the Euro area and Japan, the ECB and BoJ also hiked rates 
this month. It is the first time on record all three - Fed, ECB 
and BoJ - have hiked in the same month, as they respond 
primarily to a resurgence in energy -driven inflation amid tight 
job markets. BoJ is expected to continue hiking once every 
quarter till mid -2027 as we believe the bank  is behind the 
curve and will need to speed up the pace of rate hikes.
